## Page 5


Foreword
The p-adic Simpson correspondence was introduced by Gerd Faltings in his seminal 2005 article
[12]. It draws on two distinct sources: a complex-geometric origin, which provides both its name
and its guiding formalism, and a p-adic origin, namely Sen theory, which shares its central objective,
the description of p-adic representations of the fundamental group of an algebraic variety over
a p-adic field, as well as the techniques of p-adic Hodge theory. Since then, this program has
revealed a wide array of facets and incarnations (geometric Sen theory, analytic variants, etc.), as
well as several complementary approaches (Fontaine-type correspondences via various period rings,
crystalline, prismatic, among others). Its role within p-adic Hodge theory continues to stimulate
many significant developments, including contributions of the present authors [4, 3, 30] and,
without any claim to completeness, recent works by Bhargav Bhatt and Mingjia Zhang, Tongmu
He [18], Ben Heuer [19], Ben Heuer and Daxin Xu [20], Lue Pan [26], Juan Esteban Rodríguez
Camargo [10], Daxin Xu [32], among others.
The main goal of this book is to establish a robust framework for studying the functoriality
of the p-adic Simpson correspondence. We introduce a new method for twisting Higgs modules
via Higgs–Tate algebras. This construction builds on one of our earlier approaches to the p-adic
Simpson correspondence [4], which it recovers as a special case. The resulting framework yields
twisted pullbacks and higher direct images of Higgs modules, thereby enabling a systematic study
of the functoriality of the p-adic Simpson correspondence under arbitrary pullbacks and proper
(log)smooth direct images, including for morphisms that do not admit liftings to the infinitesimal
deformations used in the construction of the correspondence.
The core of our approach is the introduction of a twisting functor for Higgs modules, developed
in the general setting of a ringed topos (X, OX), where OX is p-adically complete and separated,
and flat over a complete valuation ring of height 1, with algebraically closed fraction field of
characteristic 0 and residue field of characteristic p > 0. Starting from an exact sequence of locally
free OX-modules of finite type
0 →OX →F →E →0,
we associate to it a Higgs–Tate OX-algebra, defined as the direct limit
C = lim
−→
n≥0
Symn
OX(F),
which may be viewed as a twisted form of the symmetric algebra of E . Inspired by our previous
construction of the p-adic Simpson correspondence [3, 4], we define an endofunctor
τ: HM(OX, E ) →HM(OX, E ),
on the category of Higgs OX-modules with coefficients in E , together with an admissibility condition
defining the class of (weakly) twistable Higgs modules. The study and applications of this functor rely
on a Poincaré lemma; accordingly, the construction is carried out using a weak p-adic completion of
C .

## Page 6


6
FOREWORD
This twisting functor admits several fundamental applications.
First, it provides a systematic construction of twisted pullbacks and higher direct images for
Higgs modules. The twisted pullback for Higgs modules was introduced by Gerd Faltings in [12]
through a different construction, and plays a central role in his treatment of the p-adic Simpson
correspondence for non-small generalized representations via a descent argument. Twisted higher
direct images for Higgs modules, on the other hand, do not appear in Faltings’ work, nor elsewhere
in the literature, and, to the best of our knowledge, are introduced here for the first time. They
generalize, on relative Dolbeault cohomology, the Higgs-field analogue of the Katz–Oda construction
of the Gauss–Manin connection on relative de Rham cohomology.
Second, the twisting functor yields a new construction of the p-adic Simpson correspondence
for small Higgs bundles, encompassing and refining our earlier work [3, 4].
Finally, it provides the key tool for establishing functoriality properties of the p-adic Simpson
correspondence, improving our earlier results obtained under strong liftability assumptions [3]. These
properties are derived from compatibility results for twisting functors, notably the identification
of the composition of two such functors with a single one via a homomorphism between the
corresponding Higgs–Tate algebras.
The extensions underlying the construction arise naturally from deformation theory. More
precisely, given a commutative diagram of morphisms of (logarithmic) schemes

where ι is a first-order thickening, ef is smooth, and the lower square and the exterior rectangle are
Cartesian, one considers the torsor of local liftings of h over e
X. This torsor is affine under a vector
bundle naturally expressed in terms of relative differentials, and its module of affine functions is an
extension of the required form, from which the Higgs–Tate algebra, and hence the twisting functor,
are constructed.
In this way, each twisting functor is ultimately governed by first-order thickenings. In the case
of the p-adic Simpson correspondence, the morphisms ι and j are given by Fontaine’s universal
p-adic first-order thickenings, while i is an auxiliary choice on which the correspondence depends.
This dependence makes the functoriality properties of the p-adic Simpson correspondence more
delicate and involved.
This book is organized as follows. Chapter 1 contains a detailed overview of our results, together
with a digression illustrating the relevance of our twisting approach in the setting of classical Sen
theory (1.9).
In Chapter 2, we gather a number of technical preliminaries that are essential for the sequel.
These go well beyond the p-adic framework, and most are original, including the theory of quasi-
fibered categories (2.11), which is of independent interest.
Chapter 3 is devoted to the central notion of this work, namely the twisting of Higgs modules,
and introduces the corresponding twisted pullbacks and twisted higher direct images of Higgs
modules.

FOREWORD
7
Finally, Chapter 4 contains our main results: the new construction of the p-adic Simpson
correspondence by twisting (for small objects) and its compatibility with twisted pullbacks and
twisted proper (log-)smooth higher direct images.

CHAPTER 1
An overview
1.1. Introduction
1.1.1.
Initiated by Faltings [12] and subsequently developed through various approaches,
including two due to the authors [4], the p-adic Simpson correspondence establishes an equivalence
of categories between certain p-adic étale local systems on an algebraic variety over a p-adic field
and certain Higgs bundles. This theory has two distinct origins: one in complex geometry and the
other in the p-adic Hodge theory. From the complex Simpson correspondence it inherits its name
and guiding formalism, while the p-adic origin supplies the techniques and the main tools.
1.1.2.
With some partiality, yet in good conscience, one may trace the immediate origin of
the p-adic Simpson correspondence to Sen theory [27]. Let K be a complete discrete valuation field
of characteristic 0, with perfect residue field k of characteristic p > 0, OK the valuation ring of K,
K an algebraic closure of K, OK the integral closure of OK in K, GK the Galois group of K over
K, OC the p-adic Hausdorff completion of OK, mC its maximal ideal, C its field of fractions.
Let χ: GK →Z×
p be the cyclotomic character of K, describing the action of GK on the p-power
roots of unity in K. Let log: Z×
p →Zp denote the usual p-adic logarithm, defined for all x ∈1+pZp
by log(x) = P

Zp-extension of K contained in K, i.e., the unique Zp-extension of K contained in the subfield of
K generated by the p-power order roots of the unity. We denote by L the closure of K∞in C.
A C-representation of GK is a C-vector space equipped with a semilinear action of GK. We
denote by RepC(GK) the category of finite-dimensional continuous C-representations of GK.
Similarly, we define the categories RepK∞(Γ) and RepL(Γ).
Theorem 1.1.3 (Sen, [27] Theorems 2 and 3). The functors
RepK∞(Γ) →RepL(Γ),
V 7→V ⊗K∞L,
(1.1.3.1)

The functor that associates to a C-representation W of GK the L-representation W H of Γ is a
quasi-inverse of the second functor. Sen constructed an explicit quasi-inverse of the first functor
([27] Theorem 3).
Theorem 1.1.4 (Sen, [27] Theorem 4). For every finite-dimensional continuous K∞-repre-
sentation V of Γ, there exists a unique K∞-endomorphism σ of V satisfying the following property:
for every x ∈V , there exists an open subgroup Γx of Γ such that for every g ∈Γx, we have
(1.1.4.1)
g(x) = exp(log(χ(g))σ)(x).
Moreover, there exists a K∞-basis of V with respect to which the matrix of σ has coefficients in K.

1. AN OVERVIEW
1.1.5.
For any field extension F of K, a Sen F-module is a pair (V, σ) consisting of a finite
dimensional F-vector space V equipped with an F-linear endomorphism σ: V →V . If (V, σ) and
(V ′, σ′) are two Sen F-modules, a morphism from (V, σ) to (V ′, σ′) is an F-linear map f : V →V ′
such that f ◦σ = σ′ ◦f. Sen F-modules form a category, denoted by SMF , which is canonically
equivalent to the category of F[X]-modules having finite dimension over F.
Theorems 1.1.3 and 1.1.4 provide an exact and faithful functor
(1.1.5.1)
RepC(GK) →SMK∞,
W 7→(V, σ).
Moreover, (V, σ) determines W up to an isomorphism ([27] Theorem 7).
By ([27] Theorem 10), if the residue field k of OK is algebraically closed, the functor (1.1.5.1)
descends into a non-canonical equivalence of categories
(1.1.5.2)
RepC(GK)
∼
→SMK.
1.1.6.
The p-adic Simpson correspondence may be viewed as an analogue of the functor
(1.1.5.1). It describes certain p-adic representations of the geometric fundamental group of an
algebraic variety over K, admitting a suitable integral model, in terms of linear algebra objects,
namely Higgs modules.
1.1.7.
In this book, we introduce a new method for twisting Higgs modules using Higgs–Tate
algebras. This construction is inspired by one of our earlier approaches to the p-adic Simpson
correspondence, which it recovers as a special case. The resulting framework provides twisted
pullbacks and twisted higher direct images of Higgs modules, allowing us to study the functoriality
of the p-adic Simpson correspondence and to generalize the results of [3], which required a strong
liftability condition on the morphism. More precisely, we establish the functoriality of the p-adic
Simpson correspondence under arbitrary pullbacks and under proper (log-)smooth direct images
by morphisms that do not necessarily lift to the infinitesimal deformations of the varieties used
in the construction of the correspondence. We also clarify how this new twisting relates to the
constructions of Heuer [19] and Heuer–Xu [20], involving line bundles on the spectral variety
(4.8.34).
In this first chapter, we give an overview of our twisting approach to the p-adic Simpson
correspondence, which we illustrate in 1.9 by providing a quasi-inverse of Sen’s equivalence of
categories (1.1.5.2).
The book treats the case of OK-schemes with toric singularities using logarithmic geometry; for
simplicity, we confine this overview to the smooth case.
Acknowledgments. This work was supported by JSPS Grant-in-Aid for Scientific Research
(Grant Numbers 20H01793 and 24K00518) and by a JSPS Invitational Fellowship for Research in
Japan (S24045), which supported the first author’s visit to the Graduate School of Mathematical
Sciences, the University of Tokyo, in spring 2024.
1.2. Twisting Higgs modules
1.2.1.
In this section, V denotes a complete valuation ring of height 1, with algebraically closed
fraction field of characteristic 0 and residue field of characteristic p > 0. We choose a compatible
system (βn)n>0 of nth roots of p in V . For any rational number ε > 0, we set pε = (βn)εn where
n is an integer > 0 such that εn is an integer. We equip V with the p-adic topology and set
S = Spf(V ).

1.3. Torsors of liftings and Higgs–Tate algebras
1.3.1.
We will apply the twisting functor τ (1.2.4.2), introduced in the previous section,
relatively to extensions (1.2.2.1) arising from deformation theory. We first explain the construction
of these extensions in an abstract setting of smooth schemes, before specializing to particular
deformations that are geometrically or arithmetically meaningful. For simplicity, we restrict in this
section to ordinary schemes; logarithmic schemes will be treated in §2.9. Consider a commutative
diagram of morphisms of schemes (without the dotted arrow)

1.7. Revisiting the global p-adic Simpson correspondence
1.7.1.
In this section, we take again the assumptions and notation of 1.5.1 and let X be a
smooth S-scheme of finite type. The p-adic Simpson correspondence constructed in §1.5 does not
readily glue to yield a global correspondence for schemes that are not small affine. To remedy this,
we sheafify the Higgs–Tate algebra in the Faltings topos of the canonical morphism h: Xη →X
and use it to construct a global p-adic Simpson correspondence, parallel to the local construction.
For small affine schemes, this global correspondence is equivalent to the local one ([3] 4.8.31 and
4.8.32).
Let ι: ˇS →eS be Fontaine’s universal p-adic first order thickening (1.5.2.2). We suppose that
there exists a smooth eS-deformation e
X of ˇX = X ×S ˇS that we fix:

by endowing each fiber category with the étale topology. We call it the Faltings (covanishing)
fibered site associated with h; see 4.7.4.
We endow E with the covanishing topology associated with π, that is, with the topology
generated by the coverings {(Vi →Ui) →(V →U)}i∈I of the following two types:
(v) Ui = U for every i ∈I, and (Vi →V )i∈I is an étale covering.
(c) (Ui →U)i∈I is an étale covering and Vi = Ui ×U V for every i ∈I.
The resulting covanishing site E is also called the Faltings site associated with h. We denote by eE
the topos of sheaves of sets on E, called the Faltings topos associated with h ([4] VI.10.1, [3] 4.3.2);
see 4.7.5. By ([4] VI.5.11), eE is canonically equivalent to the category of collections F = {U 7→FU}

## Page 43


CHAPTER 2
Preliminaries
2.1. Notation and conventions
2.1.1.
Throughout this book, we fix a universe U with an element of infinite cardinality. A
set is said to be U-small (or, when no confusion arises, small) if it is isomorphic to an element of U.
We also use the terminology: small group, small ring, small category... We say that a category C is
a U-category if for all objects X, Y of C , the set HomC (X, Y ) is U-small ([9] I 1.1). The category
of U-sets, denoted by Set, is the category of sets that are in U. It is a punctual U-topos ([9] IV
2.2). We denote by Sch the category of schemes that are elements of U. Unless explicitly stated
otherwise, it will be understood that the rings and the logarithmic schemes (and in particular the
schemes) considered in this book are elements of the universe U.
2.1.2.
Let C be a U-category ([9] I 1.1). We denote by b
C the category of presheaves of U-sets
on C , that is, the category of contravariant functors on C with values in Set ([9] I 1.2), and by
(2.1.2.1)
hC : C →b
C ,
X 7→(hC (X): Y 7→HomC (Y, X)),
the canonical functor ([9] I 1.3), which is fully faithful ([9] I 1.4). If C is endowed with a topology
([9] II 1.1), we denote by e
C the topos of sheaves of U-sets on C ([9] II 2.1).
For an object F of b
C , we denote by C/F the following category ([9] I 3.4.0). The objects of C/F
are the pairs consisting of an object X of C and a morphism u from X to F. If (X, u) and (Y, v)
are two objects, a morphism from (X, u) to (Y, v) is a morphism g: X →Y such that u = v ◦g.
2.1.3.
Let X be a U-topos ([9] IV 1.1.2). Inverse systems of objects of X indexed by the
ordered set of natural numbers N, form a topos that we denote by XN◦. We refer to ([4] III.7) for
useful facts on this type of topos. We recall, in particular, that we have a morphism of topos
(2.1.3.1)
λ: XN◦→X,
whose pullback functor λ∗associates with any object F of X the constant inverse system of value F,
and whose direct image functor λ∗associates with any inverse system its inverse limit ([4] III.7.4).
2.1.4.
Let T be a U-topos, u: F →G a group homomorphism of T, P a right F-torsor of
T. The u-twist of P is the contracted product P ∧F G, where F acts on the left on G by left
translations, i.e., the quotient of the product P × G by the group F acting diagonally:
(2.1.4.1)
P × G × F →P × G,
(p, g, f) 7→(pf, u(f−1)g);
it is naturally a right G-torsor ([14] III 1.4.6). We say that P ∧F G is obtained from P by extension
of its structural group by u or simply that P ∧F G is the u-twist of P.

2.1. NOTATION AND CONVENTIONS

2.1.7.
Let X be a scheme. We denote by ´Et/X the étale site of X, i.e., the full subcategory
of Sch/X made up of the étale schemes over X, endowed with the étale topology; it is a U-site ([9]
II 3.0.2). We denote by X´et the étale topos of X, that is, the topos of sheaves of U-sets on ´Et/X.
We denote by ´Etcoh/X the full subcategory of ´Et/X made up of étale schemes of finite
presentation on X, endowed with the topology induced by that of ´Et/X; it is a U-small site. If X
is quasi-separated, the restriction functor from X´et to the topos of sheaves of U-sets on ´Etcoh/X is
an equivalence of categories ([9] VII 3.1 and 3.2).
We denote by ´Etf/X the full subcategory of ´Et/X made up of finite étale schemes on X,
endowed with the topology induced by that of ´Et/X; it is a U-small site. The finite étale topos
of X, denote by Xf´et, is the topos of sheaves of U-sets on ´Etf/X (see [4] VI.9.2). The canonical
injection ´Etf/X →´Et/X induces a morphism of topos
(2.1.7.1)
ρX : X´et →Xf´et.
We denote by Xzar the Zariski topos of X, and by
(2.1.7.2)
uX : X´et →Xzar
the canonical morphism ([9] VII 4.2.2). If F is a quasi-coherent OX-module of Xzar, we denote by
ι(F) the sheaf of X´et defined for any étale X-scheme U by

(2.3.2.1)
θ: M →M ⊗A Ω
is SA(T)-linear (2.3.1.11).
(ii) The A-module underlying M ⊗SA(T) M ′ is the maximal quotient of M ⊗A M ′ on which
the Higgs A-fields θ ⊗idM ′ and idM ⊗θ′ induce the same Higgs A-field with coefficients
in Ω, namely its canonical Higgs field.
(i) Indeed, the Higgs A-field θ is the composition of the morphisms
(2.3.2.2)
M →M ⊗A T ⊗A Ω→M ⊗A SA(T) ⊗A Ω→M ⊗A Ω,
the first being induced by the section idΩof T ⊗A Ωidentified with H omA(Ω, Ω) (2.3.1.10), the
second being induced by the canonical injection T →SA(T) and the third being induced by µ.
Each of these morphisms is obviously SA(T)-linear.
(ii) The question being local, we may assume that Ωis a direct factor of a free A-module of finite
type Ω′. Then θ (resp. θ′) is determined by its coordinates θi ∈EndA(M) (resp. θ′
i ∈EndA(M ′))
relatively to a basis of Ω′, which commute to each other. The A-module underlying M ⊗SA(T) M ′
is the quotient of M ⊗A M ′ by the images of θi ⊗idM ′ −idM ⊗θi, for all i, which implies the
proposition.
Definition 2.3.3. Let Ωbe an A-module. A Higgs A-bundle with coefficients in Ωis a Higgs
A-module (M, θ) with coefficients in Ωsuch that the A-module M is locally projective of finite
type ([3] 2.1.11).
Definition 2.3.4. Let B be a commutative A-algebra, Ωan A-module, δ: B →Ω⊗A B an
A-derivation, which is also a Higgs A-field on B with coefficients in Ω.
(i) A B-module with δ-connection is a pair (M, ∇) consisting of a B-module M and an
A-linear morphism
(2.3.4.1)
∇: M →Ω⊗A M
such that for every local sections ξ of B and m of M, we have
(2.3.4.2)
∇(ξm) = δ(ξ) ⊗B m + ξ∇(m).
We then say that ∇is a δ-connection on M. The δ-connection ∇is said to be integrable
if it is a Higgs A-field on M with coefficients in Ω.
(ii) Let (M, ∇), (M ′, ∇′) be two B-modules with δ-connection. A morphism from (M, ∇) to
(M ′, ∇′) is a B-linear morphism u: M →M ′ such that (id ⊗u) ◦∇= ∇′ ◦u.
The B-modules with δ-connection form a category that we denote by MCδ(B/A). We denote
by MICδ(B/A) the full subcategory made of B-modules with integrable δ-connection.

exists a unique A-linear morphism
(2.3.6.1)
∇′ : M ⊗B B′ →Ω′ ⊗A M ⊗B B′
such that for all local sections m of M and ξ′ of B′, we have
(2.3.6.2)

It is a δ′-connection on M ⊗B B′. If ∇is integrable, so is ∇′.
2.3.7.
We take again the assumption and notation of 2.3.4. A δ-isoconnection with respect to
the extension B/A (or simply a δ-isoconnection when there is no risk of confusion) is a quadruple
(2.3.7.1)
(M, N, u: M →N, ∇: M →Ω⊗A N),
where M and N are B-modules, u an isogeny of B-modules ([3] 2.9.1), and ∇is an A-linear
morphism such that for all local sections x of B and t of M, we have
(2.3.7.2)
∇(xt) = δ(x) ⊗u(t) + x∇(t).
For every B-linear morphism v: N →M for which there exists an integer n such that u◦v = n·idN
and v ◦u = n · idM, the pairs (M, (id ⊗v) ◦∇) and (N, ∇◦v) are B-modules with (nδ)-connections
(2.3.4), and u is a morphism from (M, (id ⊗v) ◦∇) to (N, ∇◦v).
The δ-isoconnection (M, N, u, ∇) is said to be integrable if there exist a B-linear morphism
v: N →M and an integer n ̸= 0 such that u ◦v = n · idN, v ◦u = n · idM and the (nδ)-connections
(id ⊗v) ◦∇on M and ∇◦v on N are integrable in the sense of 2.3.4(i).
Let (M, N, u, ∇), (M ′, N ′, u′, ∇′) be two δ-isoconnections. A morphism from (M, N, u, ∇)
to (M ′, N ′, u′, ∇′) consists of two B-linear morphisms α: M →M ′ and β : N →N ′ such that
β ◦u = u′ ◦α and (id ⊗β) ◦∇= ∇′ ◦α.
We denote by IMCδ(B/A) the category of δ-isoconnections and by IMICδ(B/A) the subcate-
gory of integrable δ-isoconnections.

2.5. Ind-modules and ind-algebras
2.5.1.
In this section, we fix a ringed U-topos (X, A) (2.1.5). We denote by Mod(A) the
category of A-modules, by Ind-Mod(A) the category of ind-A-modules ([3] 2.7.1) and by
(2.5.1.1)
ιA : Mod(A) →Ind-Mod(A)
the canonical functor ([3] 2.6.3) which is fully faithful. When there is no risk of ambiguity, we
identify Mod(A) with a full subcategory of Ind-Mod(A) by the functor ιA that we omit from the
notation.
Small inverse limits are representable in Ind-Mod(A) and ιA commutes with these limits ([22]
6.1.17(ii)). Small filtered direct limits are representable in Ind-Mod(A) ([22] 6.1.8), however, ιA
does not commute in general with direct limits. We will use the notation “lim
−→” to denote direct
limits in Ind-Mod(A) and we will keep the notation lim
−→to denote direct limits in Mod(A).
The category Ind-Mod(A) is abelian and the functor ιA is exact ([22] 8.6.5). The functor ιA
admits a left adjoint ([22] 6.3.1)

The canonical morphism κA ◦ιA →idMod(A) is an isomorphism. The functor κA is exact ([3] 2.7.2).
There is a canonical tensor product ([3] (2.7.1.4))
(2.5.1.4)
⊗A : Ind-Mod(A) × Ind-Mod(A) →Ind-Mod(A),
that extends the usual tensor product of Mod(A). It makes Ind-Mod(A) into a symmetric
monoidal category, with A as the unit object.
Definition 2.5.2. An ind-A-algebra is an algebra of the monoidal category (Ind-Mod(A), ⊗A).
If B is an ind-A-algebra, a left ind-B-module is a left module over B in this monoidal category.
Ind-A-algebras form naturally a category that we denote by Ind-Alg(A). We denote by Alg(A)
the category of A-algebras. The functor ιA induces a fully faithful functor that we denote also by
(2.5.2.1)
ιA : Alg(A) →Ind-Alg(A).
When there is no risk of ambiguity, we identify Alg(A) with a full subcategory of Ind-Alg(A) by
the functor ιA that we omit from the notation.
For every ind-A-algebra B, left ind-B-modules form naturally a category that we denote by
Ind-Mod(B). It is an abelian category, and the forgetful functor Ind-Mod(B) →Ind-Mod(A)
is exact.

U(F) will also be

The extension by zero functor jU! : Mod(A|U) →Mod(A) is exact and faithful ([9] IV 11.3.1).

U. Therefore, the functor Ij∗
U commutes with representable
direct and inverse limits and is in particular exact.

(i) We denote by FA (resp. FB) the full subcategory of Mod(A) (resp. Mod(B)) made up
of flabby modules ([9] V 4.1). Injective A-modules being flabby, the category FA is cogenerating
in Mod(A). By ([9] V 5.2), the objects of FA are acyclic for f∗. Hence, the category FA is
f∗-injective by ([22] 13.3.8). Since f∗(FA) ⊂FB by ([9] V 4.9), the proposition follows by ([22]
13.3.13).
(ii) We denote by FA,Q (resp. FB,Q) the full subcategory of ModQ(A) (resp. ModQ(B)) made
up of the images by QA (resp. QB) of flabby modules. The category FA,Q is cogenerating in
ModQ(A) and the objects of FA,Q are acyclic for fQ∗. Hence, the category FA,Q is fQ∗-injective
by ([22] 13.3.8). Moreover, we have fQ∗(FA,Q) ⊂FB,Q by ([9] V 4.9). The proposition follows
then by ([22] 13.3.13).
(iii) The category Ind(FA) is If∗-injective by ([22] 15.3.2). We have αA(FA,Q) ⊂Ind(FA)
and If∗(Ind(FA)) ⊂Ind(FB). The proposition follows then by ([22] 13.3.13).

2.10. Revisiting continuous functors
Definition 2.10.1. Let C , D be two categories, φ: C →D a functor. We say that φ is a
U-functor if for every X ∈Ob(C ) and Y ∈Ob(D), HomD(φ(X), Y ) is U-small (2.1.2).
If D is a U-category ([9] I 1.1), then every functor φ: C →D is a U-functor.
Let ψ: D →D′ be a fully faithful functor. Then φ: C →D is a U-functor if and only if so is
ψ ◦φ: C →D′.

Data (ii) and (iv) are obviously equivalent. Data (i) and (ii) are equivalent because ι, ι(0) and ι(1)
are equivalences of categories by 2.11.24, and (2.11.30.1) is strictly commutative.

Definition 2.11.42 ([4] §VI.5 and [3] 2.10.1). A covanishing fibered site is a cleaved and
normalized fibered category κ: D →J ([15] VI 7.1) equipped with a topology on J and for every
j ∈Ob(J ), a topology on the fiber category Dj, satisfying the following conditions:
(i) The base J is a U-site and fiber products are representable in J .
(ii) For every j ∈Ob(J ), Dj is U-small and admits finite inverse limits.
(iii) For every morphism f : j′ →j of J , the inverse image functor f + : Cj →Cj′ is continuous
and left exact. It therefore defines a morphism of topos that we (abusively) denote also
by f : e
Cj′ →e
Cj ([9] IV 4.9.2); we denote by f s (resp. fs) the associated pullback (resp.
direct image) functor.
Every covanishing fibered site is clearly a quasi-fibered category (2.11.16) and a quasi-fibered
site (2.11.39). We can then use the notation of 2.11.1 and 2.11.36, which are compatible with the
notation above.
2.11.43.
Let κ: D →J be a covanishing fibered site (2.11.42). Following ([4] VI.5.3), The
covanishing topology of D is the topology generated by the families of coverings (Xn →X)n∈Σ of
the following two types:
(v) There exists j ∈Ob(J ) such that (Xn →X)n∈Σ is a covering family of Dj.
(c) There exists a covering family of morphisms (fn : jn →j)n∈Σ of J such that κ(X) = j
and for every n ∈Σ, Xn is isomorphic to f +
n (X).


## Page 143


2.11. QUASI-FIBERED CATEGORIES
143
The coverings of type (v) are called vertical, and those of type (c) are called Cartesian. The resulting
site is called the covanishing site associated with the covanishing fibered site κ; it is a U-site. The
associated topos of sheaves of U-sets is called the covanishing topos associated with the covanishing
fibered site κ.

CHAPTER 3
Twisting Higgs modules
3.1. Twistable Higgs modules
3.1.1.
In this chapter, V denotes a complete valuation ring of height 1, with algebraically
closed fraction field of characteristic 0 and residue field of characteristic p > 0. We choose a
compatible system (βn)n>0 of nth roots of p in V .
For any rational number ε > 0, we set
pε = (βn)εn where n is an integer > 0 such that εn is an integer. We equip V with the p-adic
topology and set S = Spf(V ).

is p-adic (i.e., p-adically complete and separated) and V -flat (i.e., p-torsion free), and an exact
sequence of locally free OX-modules of finite type

Definition 3.1.24. We say that a Higgs OX[ 1
p]-module with coefficients in E is twistable by
the extension (3.1.2.1) (or simply twistable if there is no risk of ambiguity) if it is associated with
an OX-module (3.1.23).
Remark 3.1.25. The notions introduced in 3.1.23(ii) and in 3.1.24 are clearly stable by
restriction to any object of X (3.1.3). We prove in 3.1.29 that the second one is in fact local under
extra conditions.

3.4. Coherent modules up to isogeny on formal schemes
3.4.1.

## Page 229


CHAPTER 4
Functorial aspects of the p-adic Simpson correspondence
4.1. Assumptions and notation
The conventions and notation of §2.1 are in force in this chapter, to which we add the following.
4.1.1.
In this chapter, K denotes a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic 0, with
algebraically closed residue field k of characteristic p > 0, OK the valuation ring of K, K an
algebraic closure of K, OK the integral closure of OK in K, mK the maximal ideal of OK and GK
the Galois group of K over K. We denote by OC the p-adic completion of OK, mC its maximal
ideal, C its field of fractions and v its valuation, normalized by v(p) = 1. For any abelian group A,
we denote by bA its p-adic completion.
We set S = Spec(OK), S = Spec(OK), ˇS = Spec(OC) and S = Spf(OC). We denote by s
(resp. η, resp. η) the closed point of S (resp. generic point of S, resp. generic point of S). We equip
S with the logarithmic structure MS defined by its closed point, and S and ˇS with the logarithmic
structures MS and MˇS pullbacks of MS.
For any integer n ≥1, we set Sn = Spec(OK/pnOK). For any S-scheme X, we set
(4.1.1.1)
X = X ×S S,
ˇX = X ×S ˇS
and
Xn = X ×S Sn.
4.1.2.
Since OK is a non-discrete valuation ring of height 1, it is possible to develop the
α-algebra (or almost-algebra) on this ring ([4] § V, [2] 2.6-2.10). We choose a compatible system
(βn)n>0 of nth roots of p in OK. For any rational number ε > 0, we set pε = (βn)εn where n is an
integer > 0 such that εn is an integer.
4.1.3.
With the notation of ([3] 3.1), we denote by (eS, MeS) one of the following two schemes
(4.1.3.1)
(A2(S), MA2(S))
or
(A ∗
2 (S/S), MA ∗
2 (S/S));
the first case will be called absolute and the second one will be called relative (see [3] 3.1.14).
Observe that the relative setting depends on the choice of a uniformizer π of OK and a sequence
(πn)n≥0 of elements of OK such that π0 = π and πp
n+1 = πn for all n ≥0. We have a canonical
strict closed immersion
(4.1.3.2)
ι: (ˇS, MˇS) →(eS, MeS),

generated by the canonical element eξ = ξ or eξ = ξ∗

relative case (see [3] 3.1.4 and 3.1.6). The ideal eξOeS is a free OˇS-module with basis eξ. We denote it
by eξOˇS and denote by eξ−1OˇS its OˇS-dual.
For any ringed topos (X, OX) over (ˇS, OˇS), any OX-module M and any integer i ≥1, we denote
the OX-modules M ⊗OˇS (eξOˇS)⊗i and M ⊗OˇS (eξ−1OˇS)⊗i simply by eξiM and eξ−iM, respectively.

Similar canonical Higgs fields were introduced by Camargo on pro-étale vector bundles ([10]
1.0.3), and by He in his Sen theory ([18] 5.28). They were subsequently used by Heuer [19] and
Heuer-Xu [20] in their constructions of the p-adic Simpson correspondence.

Remark 4.8.34. In the approach of Heuer [19] and Heuer–Xu [20], for a smooth proper rigid
variety, the p-adic Simpson correspondence is defined by twisting by a line bundle on the spectral
variety, in a manner similar to 4.8.33, even beyond the small case.

4.12. Relative Faltings topos. Cohomologies of Higgs–Tate algebras
4.12.1.
The assumptions and notation of §4.9 and §4.11 remain in force throughout this
section. In particular, the morphism g: (X′, MX′) →(X, MX) (4.9.1.1) is assumed to be smooth
and saturated. We denote, moreover, by G the relative Faltings U-site associated with the pair of
morphisms (h: X
◦→X, g: X′ →X) introduced in ([2] 3.4.1). Objects of the category underlying
G are the triples (U, U ′ →U, V →U) made up of an X-scheme U and of two morphisms U ′ →U

Remark 4.15.7. Canonical Higgs fields similar to those constructed in 4.15.6 were introduced
by Camargo on pro-étale vector bundles ([10] 1.0.3), and by He in his Sen theory ([18] 5.28).
They were subsequently used by Heuer [19] and Heuer-Xu [20] in their constructions of the p-adic
Simpson correspondence.
